905. ANN RIVERS was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of April, 1 half-pint of rum, value 2s., and 1 bedgown, value 6d., the goods of George Hammerstone, her master.
GEORGE HAMMERSTONE. I keep the White Horse, in Orchard-street - the prisoner lived with me about three months, and was in the habit of going to my bed-room, where I keep most of my liquors; in one corner was a large cask of gin, and I saw the bung had been removed; I replaced it, and made a mark on it - in a day or two I saw it had been removed again; I called her and asked her to let me look into her box - she refused; I sent for the officer - she then said she had lost her key, but it was opened, and better than half a pint of rum was found in a bottle wrapped up in one of my child's pinafores; I cannot swear to the rum. NOT GUILTY.

907. ANN CLARKSON and JANE TYE were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of February, 1 watch, value 2l.; 1 chain, value 2d.; 2 keys, value 1d., and 1 glazier's diamond, value 10s., the goods of Robert Poole, from his person.
ROBERT POOLE. I am a glazier. I was in the neighbourhood of Brick-lane between ten and eleven o'clock at night, on the 14th of February - I was sober; I met Tye and another woman, not in custody - they asked me for a glass of gin, as it was very cold; I said I would, but I did not know where to go - one of them, I believeTye, said, "Come in here, Sir;" they then led me into a house, and I went up five or six steps - I saw a very large fire in a grate, and I thought it was all right, but on turning round I saw a bed; I said, "This is not the place I wanted to come to" - one of them said, "Nobody will hurt you, but you will stand a drop of something to drink;" I said "I shall have no objection" - I took out sixpence, which I either put on the table or into Tye's hand; at that moment Clarkson came in, and Tye said, "The young man is going to give us something to drink;" she took the sixpence and a bottle - I said "I am off;" the other said, "You will not go" - I stopped till the gin came, and did just taste it; Clarkson then came and put her arms round me, and said, "You will not be off, stop and sleep with my neice Eliza;" I said "It is late, and I must go to my brother" - she said, "What time is it?" I pulled out my watch - Clarkson took it and ran off; I turned round, and one of the girls knocked out the candle - I said, "I see what sort of a house I am in, I will take the whole lot of you if I can" - I went into the street, and paced in front of the door till the officer came, and we went in.
WILLIAM DYKE. I heard an alarm and went up - the prosecutor said he had been robbed, and would not leave the house till he got his watch; I went in - Clarkson was not to be found, but the landlord said he would send his daughter to a house where she could most likely be found; I went backwards - Clarkson was going up stairs, and I took her; she denied having it at all - I was going to search her; she produced it from her shawl, with this diamond.
ROBERT POOLE. This watch is mine, and this diamond, which I suppose she got out of my pocket - the other two girls did not touch me.
Clarkson's Defence. He gave me the watch to look what time it was - this woman knew nothing of it; I was sitting in a chair between them - the diamond laid on the table.
CLARKSON - GUILTY. Aged 39.
Transported for Life.
TYE - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300415-242]
908 JOHN DARE was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of March, 1 purse, value 6d.; 23 sovereigns, 8 half-crowns, 20 shillings, 9 sixpences, three 10l. and one 5l. Bank notes, the property of Thomas Stanger, from his person.
THOMAS STANGER. I am a corporal in the 32nd regiment of foot. On the 11th of March I went into the City at half-past nine o'clock in the morning - I returned in the omnibus, and stopped at the Yorkshire Stingo about ten minutes; I came out and met two soldiers of the 1st regiment of Life Guards - I turned in with them to the tap next door, and remained there eight or ten minutes; I drank with them, and left the Stingo a little after eleven o'clock at night - I had been drinking a little, but not to deprive me of my senses; I was on furlough - my regiment is in Dublin; I had taken my purse out at the Stingo - any one might have seen it was bulky; I had three 10l. notes in it, one 5l. note, and twenty-three sovereigns; and the silver I had paid out of the purse for what I drank; I had received the money of my brother, who had had the keeping of it till I returned to my regiment - it was a legacy I had left me by an aunt; I had taken it out of the Bank before that - as I came out of the Stingo I saw the prisoner at the door; he asked where I lived, and being a respectable looking man I told him - he said he would show me where it was; I said I did not want him to show me, I knew it as well as he did - he said he would go as far for a walk, and when we got to where I lived, while my hands were engaged in opening the gate, he put his hand into my pocket and took the purse out; he ran up Lisson-street, and I followed him - he crossed to go down Chapel-street; I saw a Police-officer, and called out Stop thief! - I heard something fall, and I ran on to where the Policeman had got the prisoner; I said he had robbed me, and I took him back - we found the purse and all the money, within a yard of where I heard him throw it down.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Where had you been? A. To Limehouse, but I returned through the City. I got to the Stingo just at eleven o'clock, as they were closing the doors; I was quite sensible - the landlord did not refuse to draw me any more liquor; I got a quart of ale - I had nothing else; I do not recollect putting my cap on the table - I cannot swear it was not there; there were several persons there - I recollect a coachman being there; I do not recollect his taking my cap and paying half a gallon of ale for it - I do not recollect charging any one with stealing my handkerchief; I will not swear I did not - I went out between eleven and twelve o'clock; I was in the Stingo about ten minutes, and then went to the tap, but was not there above eight minutes - I do not recollect seeing the prisoner all that time: there were six or seven persons there - I have not charged any other person with this felony.
JOSEPH HAINSWORTH. I was on duty near the Yorkshire Stingo; I heard the rattle and sprang across the road - I caught the prisoner running from the prosecutor, and about eight yards in front of him; the prosecutor accused him of robbing him of his purse, but did not say what the contents of it were - he said, "I think he has flung it away," and pointed about eight yards from the place; I went there, looked, and picked up this purse - the prisoner said, "What do you catch hold of me for? I have done nothing;" I said, "What did you run for?" this is the purse.(Purse produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I beg to state that I had been to look at some goods at Mr. Flint's, being a broker and appraiser - on my return down the New-road I met a female and stood talking with her; I heard a cry of Stop him! but before that the female had told me she was the wife of a soldier, and seeing a soldier running I thought she was his wife - I ran down Chapel-street, and the Policeman crossed and took me into custody, but he never saw the soldier till he took me down Lisson-street; the soldier said, "Is that him?" - Yes, said the Policeman; the soldier was groping about and said,"Here is a purse he robbed me of" - I believe it never was out of his possession; I did not know where the Stingo was, and have not been near it - I was taken to the watch-house; the soldier was reeling about - I said,"That soldier is quite drunk, he can't charge me withrobbing him;" he said he had only had a little drop - before the Magistrate he said I had been at the Stingo and had taken him home; I should not have had a witness on my behalf had not the Magistrate issued a warrant.
COURT to HAINSWORTH. Q. Did you see any other person running? A. No, only the soldier and the prisoner; the soldier could not run as well as I could, but he dropped his cap in Lisson-street.
JURY. Q. Did you see any woman? A. No, no other person.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. If a person ran up Lisson-street, could you see him? A. Yes, it is a very open street, nearly as wide as Oxford-street; I was in Lisson-street, and in Chapel-street I caught him.
THOMAS DAVEY. I am a coachman in the employ of Mr. Waterhouse - I superintend his business at the Yorkshire Stingo, and drive occasionally. I was in the parlour, smoking a pipe with two or three coachmen, when the corporal came in with two soldiers of another regiment; it was near eleven o'clock at night - the parlour door was pushed open, and they came in and had two pots of ale; they had something at the bar, but I do not know what - I heard them say so; the corporal was very much intoxicated, and one of the other soldiers was drunk - the other was sober; the corporal had his purse out throwing about the table - the landlord advised him to put it into his pocket; he threw it about and wanted change for a 10l. note - the landlord said he had better not have change; I took up his cap which was on the table, and put it on - one of the soldiers said that was a fine of half a gallon of ale; I said I would stand a quartern of gin - the corporal then said he had lost a handkerchief; the landlord said, "How can that be when you have it in your hand?" - the prisoner was not there at all; the landlord advised the corporal not to have any more - there was a stranger there, taller than the prisoner.
COURT. Q. Did you see that he could hardly walk? A. He was beastly drunk; he could not walk steady - one of the other soldiers went out before he did, the other staid with him; the corporal then went, and the other went after him - the sober one went out first; the stranger came in as near eleven o'clock as could be, and he went out directly the soldiers did, and left his gin and water on the table - he did not return; I do not know whether he had any motive for going - we were remarking that he did not return; and I had the pleasure of drinking some of his gin and water.
JOHN NEWBANK. I keep the Yorkshire Stingo tap. I was shutting the door when the prosecutor came - he was tipsy, but he asked for pen, ink and paper: I thought it was some communication he had to make - I got a pen and ink, but he could not write at all; I said, "You had better let me write it;" he then said, "We will have a drop of rum a piece" - I gave the others a drop of rum, and then they wanted to go away, but the prosecutor took hold of them, and pushed them into the parlour, and there they had some ale; I saw the last witness put on the soldier's cap - he was to be fined half a gallon of ale, but he paid a quartern of gin; each of the soldiers took some of that, except the drunken one with the shoe on his arm, he did not drink any - the prisoner was not in the room that evening; there was a stranger there, who followed directly after the prosecutor - he was taller than the prisoner, but not so stout; I heard the prosecutor charge the last witness with stealing his handkerchief - I put my back against the door to prevent any body going out; I looked round, and said, "How can that be, when you have it in your right hand?" he then had to pay a quartern of gin for it; and he had his purse in his hand throwing it about - I said, "Put it into your pocket;" he then wanted change for a 10l. note, and I would not let him have it - he was very drunk; he could not stand still: I went to the Magistrate the day afterwards - that was the first I knew of this; the stranger went out just after the soldiers were gone - he paid me for his gin and water; I told the Magistrate I had never seen the prisoner before.
COURT. Q. The stranger had seen the bulky purse as well as you? A. Yes: the prosecutor was very drunk; he had very near knocked a person down - he might make a stumbling run; from the careless manner in which he acted in my parlour I do not think he knew what he was about: the last I saw of his purse was in his hand - he pulled it out of one of his pockets at the bar, but I cannot say what pocket; the bar is narrow: I and the soldiers persuaded him to put it away, but he knocked the soldiers about; I believe they were glad to get away: I had never seen the stranger before; there were four persons besides the soldiers.
JAMES TYDIE. I am a coachman, and was in the Yorkshire Stingo on the night in question; I saw the prosecutor and two other soldiers, but I suppose by their dress they were not of the same regiment: a friend and I were sitting smoking; the door burst open, and they all came in - nothing but the table prevented them all going down; the corporal was drunk - he sat down on the table, and took off his cap; Davey put it on, and one of the soldiers said, "It is a rule among us soldiers, that that is a fine of half a gallon of ale;" he said, "I can't stand half a gallon of ale, I will give a quartern of gin;" he gave the cap back, and then the corporal said he had lost his handkerchief out of it; the landlord said, "You have it in your hand;" the other soldier then said he should fine him a quartern of gin for accusing him of it: the prosecutor had his purse in his hand, got a fork, and opened it, and wanted change for a 10l. note, which the landlord would not give him; there was a person came in, and called for a glass of gin and water, who sat at the next table to them - he drank part of it, went out, and did not return; I did not see the prisoner in the house at all- there was but one person besides the soldiers, who were strangers to me.
COURT. Q. The corporal was very drunk? A. I considered him so - if I were so drunk I should expect to be taken off my box - I drive the Paddington omnibus; I had not driven the prosecutor - we began drinking the strangers' gin and water directly after; the house was ordered to be shut up, about a quarter of an hour or ten minutes after we drank it, and we went out immediately: I did not hear any alarm of Stop thief! I did not hear of this till the next day.
THOMAS WILSON. I live in the Harrow-road, and deal in ginger-beer. On the night of the 11th of March I was in Lisson-grove, going home, and turned down Chapel-street; I saw a tall man in a dark coat run by me, and shortly afterwards a soldier followed him - he ran up Lisson-street; I saw a man in Lisson-street talking to a woman, and pulling her about - he then let her go, and she came into the New-road; I could have stopped the man I saw running very easily, but I thought it was a frolic -I turned, and went home; I did not hear of the robbery till Friday, when I saw Tydie, and in consequence of what he said I am here to-day.
COURT. Q. Where was this man running? A. In Lisson-street; the man who was talking to the woman was on the right-hand side - he left the woman and ran; the soldier was shuffling, I could not call it a run - the man who was with the woman ran; he ran from the soldier - I could not give any reason for that; I do not know who the man with the woman was - I heard the soldier say Stop him! and I could have stopped the man who ran past me, but I thought it was a drunken frolic, and I went home; the man who ran past me was a tall man I should say - the soldier was pursuing him, then he crossed the road, and pursued the other; I did not see any Policeman.
STEPHEN COOPER. I am a coachman, and live at Paddington. On Thursday last I saw the prosecutor, as I was standing against the door of the Stingo, smoking a pipe; he accused me of being one of the prisoner's accomplices in the theft - I was at the Yorkshire Stingo on the night in question; I am well known there - it was between seven and eight o'clock last Thursday evening when the prosecutor came to me with the Policeman; the prosecutor appeared to me as drunk as any person could be, and so was the Policeman - they were not fit to walk; the Policeman had a sealing-wax pipe in his mouth, and I said it was fine times for a Policeman to have a sealingwax pipe - I said if he was not quiet I would give charge of him to another officer.
COURT. Q. What coach do you drive? A. I was driving a coach, but am out of work - I now wash coaches for a gentleman who has an omnibus; it is six or seven months since I drove a coach - I was in service seven years ago, but my master is in France; I did drive for a Mr. Trivett six or seven months ago - I used to drive odd journies: coachmen would not get so drunk as the prosecutor and the Policeman were - I was at the Stingo that very night; the prosecutor was very drunk - I did not help him home: it is the worst thing in the world to help people home who are in liquor - he could not run; he could shuffle along.
Prisoner. The Policeman who took me to the soldier was full fifty yards off me.
JOSEPH HAINSWORTH. The prosecutor was about eight yards behind him. GUILTY.
The indictment further stated that the prisoner had previously been convicted of felony.
JOSEPH STONE. I am one of the City night-patrol. I produce the copy of the conviction of the prisoner - I was a witness on the trial and know he is the man - (read.)
Prisoner. Q. Since I have been convicted has it not been clearly understood who the persons were who committed the robbery? A. No, not by me, I certainly never had an idea that you were not the person, and never said any thing of the kind.
Prisoner. I have been unjustly convicted in one instance and am sacrificed in another - he has been with the people every day. Witness. I merely waited expecting the cause to be called.
GUILTY. Aged 40. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18300415-243]
909. MARY ANN WARD was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of March, 1 blanket, value 2s.; 1 candlestick, value 2s.; 1 sheet, value 3s.; 1 rug, value 2s.; 1 bolster. value 5s.; 1 table-cover, value 2s.; 1 coverlid, value 1s., and 2 pillows, value 5s., the goods of Edward Gibbs.
EDWARD GIBBS. I live in Tonbridge-street. St. Pancras. On the 5th of October I let the prisoner a furnished lodging, as a married woman, and a night or two after she introduced a person who she said was her husband - he used to come two or three nights in a week and sleep there; I was urgent upon him, and he went away - she said he had broken his leg; I desired her to go, but she would not, and I put in a broker - this property was missing and these duplicates of it were found.
CHARLES FREDERICK FIELD. I am in the service of a pawnbroker, in Somers'-town. This property was all pawned with me by the prisoner at different times.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I had a blind mother, and I would not sit and see her starving while I had any thing to make away with.
GUILTY. Aged 25. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-244]
910. JOHN PRATT and JAMES BLYTHE were indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of February, 1 iron bar, value 2s., the goods of William Larkin; and CHARLOTTE INGLESBY was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen.
MARY LARKIN. I am the wife of William Larkin - we live in the Commercial-road. We lost an iron bar from the yard on the 22nd of February; it belonged to our shutter; Pratt lived in Grub-street, and Blythe in Glasshousefields.
HENRY SHAW. I am a Police-officer. I apprehended Pratt in Brook-street, half a mile from the prosecutor's - he had nothing with him.
EDWARD YOUNG. I am a Police-serjeant. I went with Shaw to Brook-street, to the residence of Inglesby -Pratt went to shew us the house; the woman said she had not had a bar - I asked Pratt if that was the woman; he said it was - I asked if she had sold any iron; she said, Yes, to Mr. Philpot of Love-lane; after a considerable time I told her she must have purchased the bar, as Pratt would take his oath that was the shop. and she was the person; after some time a little girl came from the parlour with this bar - the woman looked at me and said,"Oh, la! what shall I do," or words to that effect; the words were very faint.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you see her daughter? A. Yes - she appeared fifteen or sixteen years old; I do not know that she attends in the shop in her mother's absence.
WILLIAM THOMAS. I took up Blythe at his mother's house - I said, "I want you, you know something about an iron bar;" he at first denied it - I said, "We have got the boy in custody, was you with him?" he said, "I was," and stated where the bar was sold - I asked if they wenton purpose to steal it; he said No, but he carried it to be sold, and they sold it for 3d.; I had taken Pratt the evening before - he said he had stolen the bar, and he should not mind if the other boy was taken up.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not he tell you that he had fabricated a story; that he had picked it out of the water and it had fallen from a ship? A. No.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Blythe's Defence. I was going along and met this boy, who said, "Where are you going?" I said, "Home;" he said, "Here is a bit of iron, shall we take it?" I said, No - he said, "It will get a bit of bread," and he took the bar; I stood outside while he went in, and told the woman he had picked it up as a ship was unloading at the saltpetre warehouse, and it was wet - she said, "Are you sure of that?" he said, Yes; she gave him 3d. for it.
PRATT - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Confined Six Weeks.
BLYTHE and INGLESBY - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300415-245]
911. THOMAS YOUNG was indicted for embezzlement.
BENJAMIN WELLS. The prisoner was in my employ, but was not to receive money for me; he sent his brother-in-law to receive this money in my name.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300415-246]
912. THOMAS TAYLOR was indicted for embezzlement.
BENJAMIN JOSEPH. I am a broker, and live in Wardour-street - the prisoner was my shopman, and was to receive money for me, which he was to bring to me or my wife; he never accounted to me for 10l., received from Mr. Clark, who had purchased a table of me for nine guineas, and I sent it home.
WILLIAM CLARK. I purchased a table of the prosecutor, and he was to send the balance of a 10l. note - I gave the note to the prisoner.
BENJAMIN JOSEPH. He never returned, and I thought some accident might have happened to him; I went to Mr. Clark's, at Maida-hill, who showed me the receipt - I went with his mother to a lodging, which he had taken that day in St. Giles'; we waited till half-past one o'clock in the morning when he came home - his mother questioned him how he could do as he had done; he gave up the balance, 5l. 2s. 6d., which he had, and acknowledged he had bought some books, and bought a man, in his company, a new coat - he said he had spent 3l. odd, and the rest he could not give any account of; he had behaved very well till this time, and I did not wish to prosecute him had it not been on account of his obstinacy - I should think he could get employ again; he was a little in liquor- I cannot say he was drunk.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18300415-247]
913. JOSEPH BISHOP and WILLIAM WOOD were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of February, 2 bags, value 1s., and 1 bushel of turnips, value 2s., the goods of Richard Laycock, their master; and THOMAS AM-BROSE was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
MR. CRESWELL conducted the prosecution.
RICHARD LAYCOCK. I am a cowkeeper, and live at Islington; Bishop and Wood were in my employ at 18s. a week. On the 13th of February I sent them to Westham for turnips - I sent a superintendent with them, as I have lately been obliged to do; I never suffer any one to glean turnips or any thing else off my land - I expected them to return about six o'clock in the evening, but they did not; the officer came to give me some information - I have seen the nose-bags which were with them that day; I saw the turnips at the watch-house - they were the same kind as mine, but I cannot swear to them.
Cross-examined by MR. HEATON. Q. When had you seen them? A. I cannot tell; I saw them before I bought them, and I saw them afterwards - we buy them by the acre; I do not suffer people to glean - they may do it, but it is not a common thing.
COURT. Q. Did you see the turnips, said to be left at Ambrose's? A. Yes, and those which came home with the cart - they appeared to be the same sort.
THOMAS CROUCH. I am foreman to Mr. Laycock. On the 13th of February I went to West-ham, when the two prisoners went with the carts - the horses had nose-bags on their heads at Old Ford; that was the last I saw of them; they were then taken off, and tied on the shafts - the corn was very nearly all consumed then; there were three nosebags then - I afterwards missed two bags, and when I got to Dalston-lane I missed Bishop; I stopped the team, and rode back, but could see nothing of him - I went home, and heard that the prisoners were at the watch-house; these are the bags.
CHARLES HORTON PULLEY. I am a solicitor and vestry clerk, of Hackney. On the 13th of February I was returning home, down Dalston-lane - I had passed Ambrose's house about thirty yards, when I saw Bishop adjusting his nose-bag full of turnips at the back of his cart- my suspicions were excited, and I walked to within ten yards of Ambrose's house, where there is a sharp angle; I saw Wood carrying into Ambrose's house another nose-bag of turnips; I walked in through the house, into the bake-house, and saw both the prisoners there - Bishop had shot his bag of turnips, and Wood shot his; I said they must put them into the bags again, and go to the watch-house, which they did, after some hesitation - I saw a large quantity of turnips under a dresser there.
Cross-examined. Q. Did they say any thing to you? A. They said they had gleaned them off the land, but did not say where.
MR. LAYCOCK. I believe them to be mine.
Cross-examined. Q. Are they not really quite a different kind of turnip to yours? A. They really are not; there might be two days difference in the sowing of them - I had a very high opinion of these men, till I had reason to believe to the contrary.
Bishop's Defence. There were some scattered about the ground, which were not my master's; the ground was ploughed where they had been sown, and I took them up.
BISHOP - GUILTY. Aged 31.
WOOD - GUILTY. Aged 29.
Confined Three Months.
AMBROSE - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300415-248]
914. JAMES SHAW was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March, two boxes, value 20s., the goods of James Powell, and that he had been previously convicted of felony.
JAMES POWELL. I live in Prince's-street, Westminser. On the 30th of March I heard the latch of my shop-door go, as I was in my parlour; I looked into the shop and saw the prisoner walk in - my little girl went into the shop, and said "Some one will come directly," and went to ring the bell; the prisoner at that time put something in his righthand pocket, which I thought was a shell; he then said he would call again; I saw him stoop and put something into his left-hand pocket - I said Halloo, stop! he made a rush out and broke a pane of glass; I said to Mrs. Powell,"A man has run out and taken two shells in his pocket, run and call stop thief!" - in three minutes be was brought back to the shop; he said he had broken the window - I went and put my hand into his pocket, and partly pulled out a box; the officer then took him; these are my boxes - I had nothing on but my shirt and stockings at the time.
GEORGE CLIFFORD. I am the officer. I took the prisoner, and have the property. GUILTY.
ROBERT TURNER. I am an officer of St. George's, Hanover-square. I have a copy of the record of the prisoner's conviction, on the 10th of September, in the tenth year of his present Majesty's reign - I was a witness, and know he is the man. (Record read.)
GUILTY. Aged 32. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18300415-249]
915. JAMES OSBORN was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of March, 3 trowels, value 5s., the goods of Joshua Faint; and 1 trowel, value 1s., the goods of William Lewis; and that on the 18th of February last he was convicted of felony by the name of James Morell .
JAMES MANNING. I am a bricklayer's labourer. I was working at No. 6, Taylor-street, Carnaby-market, on the 15th of March, which was undergoing repair - I saw the prisoner there five minutes after six o'clock; Faint had left at three because it was wet - I was going out when the bricklayer told me to go up for his jacket; I went up and saw the prisoner in the two-pair front room on his knees, with his hat beside him - he was taking the tools out of the box and putting them into his handkerchief; I stood behind him and asked what he was doing- he said he was coming to finish this job; I said,"There are some men here besides you" - he said, "I am come to do it;" - he took a trowel and began to beat some mortar - I took him in charge; he had not been at work there.
Prisoner. You asked me what I was doing, and I said I came from Mr. Croxon, the builder; I was on my hands and knees mixing up the mortar, and you struck me. Witness. I did not.
JOSEPH FAINT. I am a journeyman bricklayer. I left at three o'clock, on account of the rain, and had put my tools away; these three trowels are mine.
WILLIAM LEWIS. I am a journeyman bricklayer. This trowel is mine. I had left it in the same box; the prisoner had no right there.
Prisoner. Faint knows me and my father and mother.
JOSEPH FAINT. Yes, I know all the family; I came from the same village in Hertfordshire. GUILTY.
JOHN LANDON. I am an officer. I got this certificate of the prisoner's former conviction from Mr. Clark's office; on the 18th of February last the prisoner was convicted of felony, by the name of James Morell - I was a witness and know he is the man - (certificate read.)
GUILTY. Aged 35. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18300415-250]
916. ALFRED GAZELEY and JOHN MUNN were indicted for stealing, on the 9th of March, 130 lbs. of pork, value 3l., the goods of John Ambler; and that, on the 16th of July, the said Alfred Gazeley, was convicted of felony.
BENJAMIN AMBLER. I am the son of John Ambler, of Ball's-pond. On the 8th of March my father killed a pig, and hung it in the front yard; I and my father sat up that night in the shed, and three other persons sat up in the stable, as we had lost three pigs before; at two o'clock in the morning the two prisoners came together - they got over two high sences into the back yard, and I saw no more of them till they were bringing this dead pig back, which had been in a new shed in the front yard; they were both carrying it - it weighed eighteen stone - we took them, and this time we saved the pig; I have seen Gazeley before.
WILLIAM SHEPPARD. I am a groom. I was in the front yard, and saw the prisoners come to the pork; Munn untied the rope, and Gazeley took it on his back - they then both carried it.
JOSEPH TODD. I was watching at the place, and took the prisoners.
Gazeley's Defence. We leave it to the mercy of the Court.
MUNN - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.
THOMAS COPE. I am an officer of Islington. I have a certificate of the former conviction of Gazeley - I took him, and was at the trial; I know he is the man.
GAZELEY - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18300415-251]
917. JOHN COOPER was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of April, 1 table, value 16s., the goods of Thomas Goode.
JAMES SWAIN. I am errand-boy to Mr. Woollard. On the 12th of April, between seven and eight o'clock, I saw the prisoner take a table from outside Mr. Goode's shop, opposite the window; I gave an alarm - he put it down at the corner of Crown-street, and ran off; there was a cry of Stop thief! and he was taken in five or ten minutes - I lost sight of him as he turned Charles-street, Pitfield-street; I am sure he is the person.
ELIZABETH ALLEN. I lodge at Mr. Thomas Goode 's, in Old Street-road. This table was near his shop-door - the witness ran up stairs to me; as I came down the table was brought into the shop by an errand-boy; I went round the corner to look for my husband, and picked up the prisoner's hat - this is the table.
JAMES SWAINE. As the prisoner was putting his head under the table, his hat fell off.
JOSEPH ELISHES. I am errand-boy to Mr. Goode. I saw the prisoner put the table down at the corner of Crown-street; I had not seen him take it up - I called out Stop thief! I did not see him caught, but saw him immediately afterwards - he dropped his hat.
JOHN JOHNSON. I was in conversation with a friend, at the corner of Crown-street; I saw a man coming with a table on his shoulder - there was a cry of Stop thief! heput it down and ran off - my friend pursued him; he was without a hat.
Prisoner's Defence. A man rushed round the corner and took off my hat - I ran after him, and a man gave charge of me.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Recommended to Mercy by the Prosecutor.
Fined One Shilling and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18300415-252]
918. ELIZABETH BUMPSTEAD was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of February, 4 pewter pot, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Edward Butt, and 1 pewter pot, value 1s. 6d., the goods of James Scull .
EDMUND BUTT. I keep the Black Cap public-house, Camden-town . Four of these pots are mine; I know nothing about the prisoner.
JAMES SCULL. I am a publican. I keep the Wheat Sheaf public-house . This pot is mine.
WILLIAM JAMIESON. I am an officer. On Wednesday, the 24th of February, I saw the prisoner in Camden-town; I followed her across the fields - I came up and stopped her; I found these five pots in a stuff petticoat under her apron - she begged me to let her go.
The prisoner made a long Defence, stating she was a widow with six children, and had not the means of providing for them.
GUILTY. Aged 37. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-253]
919. EDWARD WILLIAMS and THOMAS MORGAN were indicted for stealing, on the 18th of March, 24 stockings, value 15s., the goods of William Kipling.
THOMAS FOGG. I am a Thames Police-constable. On the 18th of March I was in Rosemary-lane; I saw the two prisoners in company - Morgan was carrying this bundle under his apron; I asked what he had there - he said, some stockings, which a person employed him to sell for him - I told him to show me the person; I went with them down the lane, and then they said it was of no use telling a lie about it, and Williams added, "We stole them out of Cheapside."
THOMAS STILL. I am in the employ of Mr. William Kipling, a hosier, in Cheapside. These are his property, and were missed, on the 18th of March, from a stool near the door, in our shop - there are twelve pairs tied up, and they have our mark on them; I did not see the prisoners.
Morgan's Defence. I was in distress, through my master being a bankrupt.
WILLIAMS - GUILTY, Aged 18.
MORGAN - GUILTY. Aged 21.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-254]
920. JOHN JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of March, 1 truck, value 3l., the goods of George Sanderson.
GEORGE SANDERSON. I live at Edmonton, this truck is mine. and was in the yard in front of my house on the evening of the 22nd of March, and the next morning at six o'clock I saw it in Bunhill-row; I know nothing of the prisoner.
GEORGE SHEPHERD. I am a Police-officer. On the 23rd of March I was on duty, and saw the prisoner coming with this truck in a direction from Kingsland-road to Shoreditch; I asked where he was going to take it - he said it belonged to a man named Brown, in Tower-street, and he was sent down to a person named Francis at Edmonton, with fourteen pieces of wood - I did not think it was a correct story, and I took him into custody: in the course of a week I found the prosecutor.
Prisoner. I did it through distress.
GUILTY. Aged 31. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18300415-255]
921. JOHN WILTSHIRE and JOHN COOKE were indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March, 1 tea-kettle, value 4s. 10d., the goods of John Northfield.
JAMES TAYLOR. I am a butcher, and live in the Cityroad. On the 26th of March I saw the two prisoners together, in Bath-street, not ten minutes walk from the prosecutor's - Wiltshire had an apron, and when I got close to them, I saw the spout of this tea-kettle; I passed them and saw them turn up a court - I went round Old-street, and saw them again; Cooke had then got the apron on, and the kettle open in his hand - I told the officer; I ran and took Cooke and Barrows took Wiltshire - I found these boots on Cooke, and a duplicate of an article pawned the same day.
JAMES BARROWS. I am an officer. Taylor pointed out the two prisoners; Cooke had an apron on, and this kettle in his hand.
ROBERT SANGSTER NORTHFIELD. I am the son of John Northfield, an ironmonger. This is his kettle, it had been outside his house in the City-road, on a stove by the window: it has my mark on it.
JOHN BEDFORD. I was serjeant of the Police. The prisoners were brought in, and charged with stealing this tea-kettle; when I was taking them to Worship-street, I heard Cooke ask Wiltshire what he should say - he said he did not know: Cooke said, "Say we found it" - Wiltshire said No; Cooke then said, "Say it was given you to carry:" and that was the defence they made before the Magistrate.
WILTSHIRE GUILTY. Aged 21.
COOKE GUILTY. Aged 24.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18300415-256]
922. WILLIAM MORRIS WOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 1 handkerchief, value 4s.; 1 waistcoat, value 10s.; 1 pair of drawers, value 2s. 6d.; 1 pair of stockings, value 1s. 6d. and 1 pair of braces, value 6d., the goods of William Plant, his master.
WILLIAM PLANT. I am a hosier, and live in Oxford-street. The prisoner was in my employ rather more than two months; for three or four weeks before I gave him in charge. I observed a particular pattern silk waistcoat which he had, and I asked him if he had been helping himself to it, from my stock; he said No, Mr. Edward Busby and he had one a piece off the same silk - this was on Sunday; and the following day I looked over my stock, and missed one of these waistcoats: I kept a sharp look on him, but detected nothing else, till the night previous to my giving him into custody, when he and the apprentice got tipsy in my absence: the following day I got a friend to come, and ask for a similar waistcoat - I said to him, "Wood it is the same as you have, let the gentleman look at that:" he brought it down after some time, and in consequence of what I had heard, I gave him into custody - the officer then searched his box, and found theseother things, which are like what I had in the shop; but they are of that general character I could not identify them.
BENJAMIN WILLIAM VALENTINE. I was sent for, and searched the prisoner's box; I found these articles, which the prosecutor said he had no doubt were his - here is a pair of stockings and a pair of gaiters; these braces are quite new - this waistcoat was brought to me by the prosecutor.
Mr. PLANT. I believe this waistcoat to be mine, and these braces he says he paid the apprentice for, who says he did not receive it; I have no such black stockings as these now, but I had such, and this handkerchief and drawers.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How many pieces of silk are there of this pattern? A. I cannot tell. These stockings I have more hesitation about than the other property; I sent the person to buy goods - the prisoner did not give me a true account of that, but I ultimately got the money.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the waistcoat at Mr. Webb's; the handkerchief I brought home from India.
JOHN RICHMOND WEBB. I cannot recognize the prisoner as purchasing any waistcoat of me, but I had this pattern in my possession; he might have bought it at some other shop.
MARY THOMAS. My husband is a labourer. I have known the prisoner twelve months; I have washed these stockings for him, before he went to the prosecutor's.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300415-257]
922. SAMUEL NEWMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of March, 1 handkerchief, value 18d., the goods of Joseph Smith, from his person.
JOSEPH SMITH. I am a solicitor. On Sunday, the 21st of March, about eleven o'clock in the morning, I was in Featherstone-street; the prisoner took the handkerchief from my pocket - I turned round, and saw it in his hand; he flung it at me and ran; I pursued, and took him about one hundred and fifty yards off.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How do you know this handkerchief? A. I think my initials are on it; here is a little hole in it - I did not feel any body at my pocket; I had my pocket-book in the same pocket.
Prisoner's Defence. There were three boys behind me; there were no others there.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Recommended to Mercy. - Whipped and Discharged.

924. RICHARD WHITING was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of February, 1 piece of coping-stone, value 2s., the goods of Thomas Want and John Richardson.
EDWARD SPARROW. I am a stone-mason. I saw the prisoner take the stone from the prosecutors' wharf, on the 19th of February, about two o'clock in the afternoon- I stopped him and asked where he was going; he said,"Just up here" - I said, "Come back, and tell me where you got it;" I took him back, and asked a man if he had allowed him to take it - he said No; I asked who sent him, and he said "A man round here;" I went round and saw two men and a woman - I said, "Was it either of them?" he said No.
JAMES RUSSELL. I was working for Mr. Spencer; I was with Sparrow when we saw the prisoner with the stone.
RICHARD DRISCOLL. I am in the employ of Want and Richardson. Mr. Sparrow called me out and asked if I had given the prisoner this stone; I said No.
JOHN RICHARDSON. I am partner with Mr. Thomas Want; this is our property.
THOMAS THOMPSON. I am an officer. I was called and took the prisoner; I asked what he was going to do with the stone - he said a man promised him a pint of beer to carry it.
Prisoner's Defence. A man called me and said, "If you will carry this stone, I will give you a pint of beer;" I took it and was stopped - the man went round the corner.
Mr. SPARROW. I saw him take it off the wharf, and there was but one way to go to it.
GUILTY. Aged 45. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18300415-260]
925. MARY TOUMEY was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of March, 1 pair of shoes, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Alexander Cowan.
LOUISA COWAN. I am the daughter of Alexander Cowan - he keeps a shop in Aylesbury-street, Clerkenwell. I was at his door on the afternoon of the 22nd of March - a person told me a pair of shoes had been taken; I sent my brother after them - I missed one pair from the corner, which I had seen two or three days before; I had not seen my father hang them at the door.
LEWIS COWAN. I am ten years of age. I found the prisoner with the shoes in her basket, and told her she must come back, which she did; I had seen the shoes that morning - my father hung them out - this was about twelve o'clock.
ALEXANDER COWAN. These are my shoes.
HENRY BERESFORD. I took the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I picked them up as I was passing the shop; the boy asked if I had picked up a pair of shoes - I said Yes; he said they were his father's - I said he should have them back.
LEWIS COWAN. She did not say how she got them - she said, "Here are your shoes, I don't want them."
JURY to ALEXANDER COWAN. Q. Could not a person going by with a basket knock them down? A. No; they were on a line, which hung close to the window - they could not be got without lifting them up, or cutting them.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300415-261]
926. SUSAN TAPE was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of February, 3 table-cloths, value 14s.; 1 apron, value 1s.; 1 cap, value 1s., and 25s. in copper monies, the property of Ambrose Charles.
MARTHA ELIZABETH CHARLES. I am the wife of Ambrose Charles. The prisoner came to assist my servant, who was ill - she came on the Monday, and staid till the Sunday night, when she absented herself; I missed this money from the parlour closet - I have seen the tablecloths, apron and cap since.
SAMUEL HASLOP. I am a pawnbroker. I have a table-cloth, a pair of woman's shoes, and a shift, pawned with me; the table-cloth on the 10th of February, by the prisoner, I believe.
THOMAS SWEATMAN . I am a grocer. I tied up these papers of halfpence, and paid them to Mr. Charles on Saturday evening, the 13th of February; I know these are the papers.
MARY COLLINS. I lived servant at the place. The prisoner took these shoes, and this collar of mine.
JOHN WHITE. I am an officer. I have a cap which I took from her, and this apron and copper money I found in a box which the landlady said was hers - these papers were about the money; one was tied up, and the other had been opened.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Transported for Seven Years.
There was another indictment against the prisoner, which was not tried.
[trialAccount t18300415-262]
927. WILLIAM STEPHENS was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of April, 2 doors, value 1l., and 1 wooden partition, value 14s., the goods of Samuel Emsley.
SAMUEL EMSLEY. I am a stay-maker, and live in Kingsland-road. I began building a manufactory seven months ago; I found all the materials - the prisoner was to find labourers, and do the carpenter's work. On Saturday, the 10th of April, I went to see if the workmen had come - I met the prisoner coming from my premises with some beads and timber - I asked him why he did not go on with his work; he said, "I do not mean to take these things, Mr. Emsley;" I said, "It looks very much like it"- he then said, "I did take them," and he went back and threw them down; I then went to another place, where he had a contract and was to find the materials - I there found two doors, which I knew belonged to me - he followed me- I said I should not leave him till I had given him in charge; he went with me to Bethnal-green, and I gave him in charge for stealing two doors, which he denied, and said he had them by him for twelve months - but at Worship-street he acknowledged he had taken them; I do not recollect the words he first used, but he said be bought them of Pain, my foreman - I knew the doors, and on the Tuesday following I found a partition that he was painting- I ordered him to desist, as it was mine; he said it was not, but he afterwards, before the Magistrate, said he bought them of my foreman.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Have you any partner in this building? A. No; the prisoner is a master carpenter - he walked with me till I gave him in charge; the exterior of the doors was the same as they were before he took them.
THOMAS PAIN. I am foreman to the prosecutor. I never sold these, or any thing else to the prisoner - I was ordered to go and take two doors which the prosecutor considered belonged to him, which I did, and gave to the officer.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you never received any money from the prisoner? A. No; I never received 12s. 6d., nor ever sold him any thing.
JOHN COLLINS. I work for the prosecutor. I know these doors are his.
JOHN COBHAM. I am in the employ of the prosecutor. I know these doors are his.
JAMES MASON. I employed the prisoner to build a cottage for me, at No. 30, Camden-gardens; he was to find the materials - that is where the doors were found; they had been there two or three weeks, and had not been altered or disfigured.
JAMES HAWKNAGE. I am an officer. I took the prisoner - he said he did not recollect who he bought them of- he had had them twelve months.
Witnesses for the Defence.
ROBERT HILL. I am a carpenter, and live at No. 10, Reliance-square, New Inn-yard, Shoreditch. I worked for the prisoner on those premises; I saw him take these things away, and Pain handed them outside the door to him - I think it was this day month; there was only the prisoner, Pain, and myself on the premises.
THOMAS PAIN. He might have seen me giving them out, but with no intention of his taking them away, nor did I know he was taking them; the frames were made to enclose the doors.
Cross-examined. Q. Do not you know they were taken away? A. Yes, but not by my orders; I gave them to be made use of.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor.
Confined Eight Months.
[trialAccount t18300415-263]
928. WILLIAM STANLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of March, 1 penknife, value 2s., the goods of William Elliott.
MARY FELTS. I am servant to William Elliott - he is a tobacconist. On the 17th of March the prisoner came into his shop, and asked the price of some tobacco; he was told the price, and while Mr. Elliott turned away, he stole a penknife off the counter, took it out of the shop, and gave it to another boy - I called the Police-officer, and brought the prisoner back; it was a good knife.
THOMAS MIDDLETON. I am the officer I took the prisoner; I found 1s. 6 1/2d. on him.
Prisoner's Defence. I went and asked the price of tobacco, came out, and went about a hundred yards; this lady came and said I had stolen a penknife - I said "I will go back."
GUILTY. Aged 15. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-264]
929. SARAH SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of April, 4 lbs. of beef, value 2s., the goods of Charles Baker.
CHARLES BAKER. I am a butcher. On the 10th of April a person told my man a woman had taken this beef from the shop; I had seen it about ten minutes before - the man brought the prisoner back, and she had my beef; I said to my man, "This is the woman I told you to take care of."
ANN BALLIS. I saw the prisoner take the beef from the shop, which she placed under her arm; she then took up another, took up her cloak at the corner, and laid that one down - I told the man, and he took her.
ADAM McDONNELL. I was on duty in Tottenham-court-road, and saw the man with the prisoner; I took her with the beef under her arm - she would not state where she lived.
GUILTY. Aged 46. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-265]
930. JOHN SKENNELL was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of April, 2 shillings, the monies of James White.
JAMES WHITE. I keep a shop in Bedfordbury. On the 10th of April, about six o'clock in the evening, I missed six half-crowns from my till - I could not imagine which way they went, as I could find no vacancy; the prisoner and two or three lads came in repeatedly, and that afternoon I marked some shillings; the prisoner took two of them from the till - my son told me he saw his hand go into the till, and gave me a hint about the prisoner, who ran out - I pursued; he ran, and threw the two shillings over his hand at me - I ran, and stopped him; a young man took up the two shillings, and gave them to me - they were two of my marked shillings; I do not know who took them up - he had not run above forty or fifty yards when he threw down the two shillings.
Cross-examined by MR. HEATON. Q. You say the young man picked up the money? A. Yes - I did not see him pick it up; the prisoner's father states that he is between nine and ten years old - there was a little boy he brought with him; I did not see him take them - no one was near the till but him - on the Saturday night he came for a halfpenny pencil; he brought a lad to ask for the pencil, while he put his hand under the counter into the till - I believe the prisoner had 3d. to buy a pound of flour at the next door; he had no flour-bag with him, but little bag with 3d. in it.
JURY. Q. What side of the counter is your till? A. It is next the window near the door - there is an opening at the end of the counter under it; there was a vacancy into which a lad could put his hand - he got into the dark corner; my counter is about ten feet long - this crevice is under the end of the counter; it was a long time before I couldfind it out - I lighted a candle three times, and was obliged to put it out again; I could not find it.
THOMAS CUSS. I heard a noise, went out, and took the prisoner and the money - the prosecutor said that he threw it away, and a young man picked it up and gave it him; the prisoner cried very much, but did not say any thing - he had no bag.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been an officer? A. Eight years; I have only sworn to what he told me - I was beadle of the parish, but I left it with a good character.
JAMES WHITE. These are the shillings; I marked them with a few scratches with a knife.
JOHN WHITE. I am the prosecutor's son. I was in the shop about ten o'clock in the morning of the 10th of April; I saw the prisoner go up towards the till, press against the counter, and feel as if feeling for the till - I suspected he had put his hand into the till, and told my father to take him; he ran off - my father ran and took him; I had discovered the crevice on the Saturday evening, which was so small that no man's hand could go into it - we did not miss money till that Saturday.
JURY. Q. Is the till at the top or the bottom of the counter? A. At the top - there has been a piece of board pulled away from the end of the counter, that confined the till.
COURT. Q. Did you try whether you could get any thing into the till? A. Not without stooping - it is under the counter; it is possible for a boy to get his hand in - the till is very long and comes up to the back of the counter; I saw the money was gone from the till - my father marked it.
Q. What is the depth of your till? A. Eight inches, and the opening about two inches.
Cross-examined. Q. And do you mean to say the boy could put his hand to the bottom of that till? A. I could nearly get my own hand in.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300415-266]
931. MARY ANN ROGERS was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of April, 1 gown, value 6d.; 3 handkerchiefs, value 1s. 3d., and 1 band-box, value 3d., the goods of John Gibbs Fox.
JOHN GIBBS FOX. The prisoner was my servant. On the 13th of April I went out in the afternoon, and when I returned at six o'clock in the evening, my wife told me something was missing; I looked and missed these articles - the handkerchiefs were mine, and the gown my wife's; the prisoner had left my service while my wife was out - I found her the next day at No. 20, Wych-street, and charged her with taking the things; she said the property in her box was mine.
JOHN NORTHAGE. I am an officer. I took this box and the prisoner in Wych-street - she told me she was induced to take them by a young man.
Prisoner's Defence. It was no such thing; I do not know any young man - I did not take the things myself.

933. HENRY POWIS was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of April, 5 pieces of wood, value 2s., the goods of William Fitze.
JOHN TAYLOR. I am a labourer, and live in Shepperton-place. Mr. Fitze has been building two houses in that place - these boards were missed from there on the Sunday; I saw them safe that morning at half-past nine o'clock - we missed them about ten; I know nothing of the prisoner.
WILLIAM HOLLIS. I am a cheesemonger, and live in Shepperton-place, New North-road. Last Sunday week I saw the prisoner watching about two empty houses - I saw him take this wood out of Mr. Fitze's house, and walk away with it; I followed and got an officer, who cried Stop thief! - he put down the wood and ran away; Miller took the wood, and Lovelock took him.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me take it out of the house? you said you saw me pick it up? A. I saw it risingup outside of the house, and he took it away - I did not see him in the house.
JAMES MILLER. I pursued the prisoner, and took him with these boards.
ROBERT LOVELOCK. I heard the cry of Stop thief! and stopped the prisoner; he said he picked the boards up in the road.
JOHN TAYLOR. These boards were nailed up against the windows to keep the wet out, on the Saturday afternoon, and on the Sunday I saw them safe - I cannot say whether they were taken down, or thrown down.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going across the field, and saw these boards laying on the ground; I took them up, and heard Stop thief! called. NOT GUILTY.

935. WILLIAM PARKER was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 3 weights, value 3s., the goods of Theophilus Price Haddock.
THEOPHILUS PRICE HADDOCK. I live in Margaret-street, Hackney-fields. I was in my kitchen in the afternoon, on the 2nd of April - I went into my shop, and saw the prisoner at the counter; he asked for a halfpenny worth of sugar - I asked if he had no more money, he said No; I said I would make him a half-penny worth; I took some paper to put it in, and missed my weights; I said."Where are my weights?" he said,
"I will go and get more money" - he ran out, I pursued him; he was stopped and brought back in ten minutes; the weights were found in the middle of the road; three were found exactly corresponding with those I lost.
FRANCIS CLARK. I am a baker. I went out of Mr. Richardson's shop and saw the prisoner running; I went and took him a hundred yards from the prosecutor's shop; I saw nothing of the weights.
JOHN RICHARDSON. Clark was in my mother's shop, I heard Stop! called - I went out and picked up three weights in the street; I gave them to Mr. Haddock.
ANN HADDOCK. I am the prosecutor's wife. I saw Richardson pick up something; he brought me the weights.
ROBERT OVERDON. I am a constable; he had nothing on him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I had a halfpenny in my pocket, but the gentleman did not feel in that pocket; I never took the weights.
GUILTY. Aged 13.

936. ANN MARSHALL was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of April, 1 coat, value 15s., the goods of William Coucher.
WILLIAM COUCHER. I am a hairdresser, and live in Tabernacle-walk. At half-past seven o'clock in the morning of the 14th of April I went to the parlour to light the fire -I thought I heard something come in; I turned, and saw a woman going out - I went into the shop and missed my coat from a peg; I went out, and saw the prisoner two doors from my house, with the coat; she said distress and poverty drove her to it - and she hoped I would not be hard with her; at the same time she was in a state of beastly intoxication.
THOMAS MANTON. I am the officer. I took the prisoner; she had been drinking.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in great distress; I met a person who gave me some liquor, which got into my head.
GUILTY. Aged 45. - Confined Two Months.

937. MICHAEL McCREA was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 1 candlestick, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Jesse Adkins.
JESSE ADKINS. I am landlord of the Laurel, Camden-town . My servant, Moore, came to me on the 20th of February - I went and missed a candlestick from the seat in the tap-room; the prisoner was there reading the newspaper - I went and rubbed him down; I felt something hard, and said, "What have you here?" - he said,"Nothing, Sir," - I opened his waistcoat, and found my candlestick.
JANE MOORE. I missed the candlestick, and told my master.
ABRAHAM LORIMER. I am an officer. I bring the candlestick - I found 4s. 3d. on the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I went into the tap-room - it was full of men; they said, "Hallo Pat!" and one of them hove the candlestick into my jacket.
