1014. JOHN ANSON was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 13 handkerchiefs, value 8s., the goods of William Brown.
WILLIAM BROWN. I am a linen-draper, and live in Beech-street, Barbican. On the 20th of February, about half-past five o'clock, I saw these handkerchiefs full two feet within the door; and about six I heard a bustle in the street, went out, and the prisoner was brought to my door with the handkerchiefs in his possession - I did not see them taken; I am certain of them - they have my shopmark on them.
FREDERICK PITE. I am a hatter, and live opposite to Mr. Brown. About half-past six o'clock I saw the prisoner, with a companion, lurking about Mr. Brown's door- I saw the prisoner take a piece of print, as I thought, from inside the door, and conceal it under his coat; I followed, and took him about four doors off with it under his coat - I also collared the other, but he struggled and got from me; he was taken at the end of the street again, but was afterwards rescued.(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOHN PARRY. I was on duty as patrol, and received the prisoner in charge.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that three boys ran by him, and dropped the goods, which he picked up, and was immediately taken.
FREDERICK PITE. I saw him take them, and saw nobody but him and his companion - I had seen them in company for ten minutes.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-349]
1015. RICHARD LOCKWOOD was indicted for a misdemeanor.
HENRY TAYLOR. I am in the employ of William Hall, glover, of Wood-street - Mr. Dosseter, of Threadneedle-street, dealt with him. On the 16th of February, about half-past five o'clock, the prisoner came to the warehouse, and said he wanted twelve pairs of red garters for Mr. Dosseter, of Threadneedle-street; I believed him, and gave him twelve pairs - they came to 14s.; he was a stranger to me - on leaving the warehouse he said he also wanted some warped net-purses, and looked out a dozen- he gave me the invoice of the first goods, and I added them to it, charging Mr. Dosseter with them; they came to 22s. - he came again the next evening, about the same time, and said the garters he had the over-night were not large enough, he wanted some larger, but Mr. Dosseter would keep those also; Mr. Hall said he had none in the warehouse, but would send them down - I went to Mr. Dosseter's to inquire, and found there was an imposition; I saw the prisoner next day at Cambridge-heath, and took him into custody - he denied knowing any thing about the charge.
Prisoner. Q. I think you stated in your examination that you went round to the different tradesmen? A. Yes, to warn them - I went to Mr. Bunnell among the rest, and he gave me your name and address; that led me to Hackney.
WILLIAM HULL. I saw the prisoner on both these occasions, and am certain of his person; I have no customer named Dosseter, but the one in Threadneedle-street - my man looked the things out; I entered them, and having a stiffener to repair for Mr. Dosseter. I said to the prisoner, "Here is a stiffener belonging to Mr. Dosseter, you may as well take it to him" - he said, "Oh, certainly," and took it; he asked for warped silk purses, which were added to the invoice - on the following evening I discovered the imposition.
WILLIAM DOSSETER. I live in Threadneedle-street, and deal with Mr. Hull: I had sent him a stiffener to repair; I know the prisoner - he was never in my service; I never sent him for these goods, and had no knowledge of his going for them - the stiffener was delivered at my house; I cannot say who by: I have known him about two years - he was in the habit of coming to my house with goods from Mr. Bunnell.
Prisoner. Q. Did you ever, in my presence, say you did business with Hull? A. Not to my knowledge - I might.
EDWIN BOWYER. The prisoner brought the stiffener to Mr. Dosseter's about a quarter to ten o'clock on the 14th of February; I took it in - he brought nothing else.
Prisoner's Defence. My Lord and Gentlemen of the Jury, - Amidst the greatest difficulties to encounter, I now enter on my defence - I do not mean to attribute perjury to the witnesses, but alledge they are under a mistaken idea of the identity of the person; here I am charged with a crime I am innocent of - look at the evidence of Taylor; he says, after he found out the fraud he went round to the tradesmen, and among others to my late master, Mr. Bunnell, who, in giving a description of my person, said he thought that he had such a person in his employ, and gave my name and address; Taylor came to the house, saw me outside, and immediately said I was the person - he, of course, anxious to bring the real perpetrator to justice, has unfortunately fixed on the innocent person; if I had obtained the goods should I have gone to Mr. Dosseter, where I am well known; for I should have been sure to be identified - with a full opinion that if you have a doubt of my guilt you will give me the benefit of it, knowing that it is better that ninety-nine guilty persons should escape than one innocent man be convicted.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-350]
First London Jury - Before Mr. Recorder.
1016. WILLIAM NETTLETON was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. WILLIAM DEVEY. I am a brass-founder, and a subscriber to St. Birde's charity-school, and one of the committee of superintendence - the prisoner was the schoolmaster. In consequence of the death of Mr. Blades, a gentleman was looking into the accounts, and found that 15l., which had been applied for to the Ironmongers' Company, had not been entered; I know from the prisoner himself that it had been applied for, for the use of the school - I inquired of the prisoner about it, and asked if he had discovered any thing further respecting it; he said he had not, but that he had received an order from Mr. Blades, who was the treasurer, to obtain the money from the Ironmongers' Company - he said the order was in writing, and he went and received the cheques from Mr. Pellatt, the clerk, on Messrs. Hankey's; that he brought it to the school in Dorset-street, and locked it in his desk on the Saturday till the Monday morning, and then went and received the money, which he took with him to Peckham, where he resided, brought it back on the Tuesday, put it in an an envelope, and delivered it at Mr. Blades' house, to Mr. Crook, his cashier, in the presence of Mr. Jones; I inquired of the prisoner two days afterwards whether he had been to Mr. Blades' to inquire about it, and he said he had not, at which I expressed my surprise - I think the day after he said Mr. Crook and Mr. Jones recollected that he had been there, but he did not say they recollected his giving the money; I afterwards found the number of the notes, and made inquiries - in consequence of what I heard I sent for the prisoner, and gave him in charge; I told him what I had discovered, but said I did not wish him to say any thing to me about it - as he was going out he stepped back, and asked if it could not some how be settled; I told him to say nothing more - I did not at all agree to settle the matter.
Cross-examined by MR. CRESWELL. Q. The prisoner was parish-schoolmaster? A. Yes, and as such his duty was merely to instruct the boys - there is a collector of the subscriptions to the school; Mr. Blades was treasurer, and used to pay the bills for the school, by order from the committee of superintendence - this gift was called Betton's gift; I believe it could not have been got without the intervention of Mr. Blades - I believe the prisoner had been schoolmaster a year and a half, or two years; I believe Mr. Blades himself received it the year before; Mr. Blades was at this time afflicted with paralysis, and I have heard was afflicted so as to be deprived of the power of speech; there is a collector, but if I had been treasurer I would not have sent him, because he has a per centage, and the schoolmaster has not - I have never received it myself: the prisoner was merely sent to receive it on account of the illness of Mr. Blades; the schoolmaster has a regular salary - there had been a gratuity made to the former schoolmaster by the committee of superintendence, but not to the prisoner; the former schoolmaster must have had an order to go to Mr. Blades for it - he had been in the situation a number of years, and had only had it at particular times; by no means every year - the prisoner had not the least right to expect it; he had not conducted himself well - the notes the prisoner received have been traced, and are here; I supposed the prisoner had made inquiry between the first time of my asking him and the second, and upon the second occasion he stated that Mr. Crook and Mr. Jones had recollected his being there - the prisoner said he had directed the letter as he was accustomed to direct them, for " J. Blades, Esq., Treasurer;" he said the money was inclosed in it, and it was left with Mr. Crook - he said he had left the money, but did not sayit was the identical notes he had received from Hankeys - I understood they were the same notes, but I might be wrong; he might have other notes and have left them -Mr. Brooks, of New-street-square has succeeded Mr. Blades, as Treasurer; this circumstance happened in July, and Mr. Blades died quite at the end of the year.
COURT. Q. Was the person whom he succeeded always rewarded with a gratuity to the same amount? A. No, my Lord, he had it but once to my remembrance, and I think it was 10l.
WILLIAM CROOK. I was in the service of Mr. Blades; I know the prisoner perfectly well - I certainly never received any money from him, or any thing stated by him to contain money from the Ironmongers' Company; he was not at all employed by Mr. Blades in his general service - I never had any conversation with him respecting any money received from the Ironmongers' Company till before his examination had taken place; he came down to ask if I did not recollect receiving the money - I said I did not; there was a Mr. Jones in Mr. Blades' service - I think the prisoner said he paid it about July; I can say with perfect recollection, and with perfect truth, that he never paid any money over to me - I was in the habit of opening all notes at that time, both private and public; I had a special power of attorney to transact any business on Mr. Blades' account, and in his name; if any money had come I ought to have known it, but Mr. Jones sometimes opened letters; I know Mr. Blades' gave the prisoner authority to go and receive the money at the proper time.
Cross-examined. Q. Can you say of your own knowledge that Mr. Jones opened all notes and letters which you did not? A. That I cannot say; Mr. Blades was in such a state that no notes relative to business certainly, and I believe no private notes were delivered to him; when Mr. Blades received money it first passed through the cashier's books, but this money never did in the business accounts - I had a good deal to do with his private accounts while he was indisposed; if I had received this money I should have entered it in the public books; the year before they did not usually come into my hands - the prisoner was not in the habit of calling on Mr. Blades; I should think he did not call for six month's together - he never left letters to my knowledge since I had authority to open them, and I should think not previous to that, because Mr. Blades was much out of town; I had many letters that he never saw.
COURT. Q. Was Mr. Blades in a state in which letters would not have been received by him at this time? A. Yes, certainly.
FRANCIS JONES. I was in the employ of the late Mr. Blades - I occasionally opened letters that came to him; I certainly did not receive any letter from the prisoner containing notes or money at that time, or any account that he had received money; he came to me and asked whether I recollected his bringing a note to Mr. Blades, directed " I. Blades, Esq., Treasurer of St. Brides' School" - I told him certainly not; I perfectly recollect that be never had - he had no general employ from Mr. Blades.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not open all letters? A. I did not; but, generally speaking, I knew the contents of all letters, at least ninety-nine out of a hundred. I am in the shop from eight o'clock till eight - there are eight or ten persons in the shop, and sixteen or eighteen persons altogether; the prisoner might have come in during my absence, and have left a letter, without its coming to my hands.
COURT. Q. Had any other person authority to open letters except Mr. Crook and you? A. There was occasionally, but very rarely; they principally came to him or me.
JOHN SILLINGHART. I am porter to the Company of Ironmongers. I produce the book to certify that the prisoner's signature is in it, and that he received the cheque; this is it.
WILLIAM HENRY WRIGHT. I am clerk to Messrs. Hankeys. I know nothing of the prisoner, but I paid a cheque for 15l. drawn by Thomas Pellatt, who was clerk to the Ironmongers' Company at that time: (read) "July 1, 1829, pay St. Bride's school, or bearer, Fifteen Pounds, Thomas Pellatt;" I paid it in three 5l. notes, Nos. 6116, June 4, and 2728, June 15; there is an error in the number of this note - I omitted a one; the real number is 27,281- the other note is No. 24,961, June 6.
COURT to MR. CROOK. Q. Did you ever receive any 5l. notes of these numbers on account of Mr. Blades? A. That is impossible to say, but if I did they most likely have my signature upon them.
JOHN HUCKLE. I am inspector of the watch in St. Bride's parish. I took the prisoner into custody; I was desired by Mr. Devey to request him to come to town, and to be as delicate as I could, on account of his wife and family- he at first refused to come, and said he would come on Monday; I then requested to speak with him, and told him my business - he then said, "I will go with you," but he made no acknowledgement to me at all.
Cross-examined. Q. I presume he knew you? A. No, he did not.
JOHN REAHDEN. I made the prisoner a pair of trousers; he gave me a 5l. note, and I charged it in the course of a few days, at a public-house; I wrote the prisoner's name on it - I have seen it since, and am quite sure it was the note I received of him some time in July last, I think.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you every make any clothes for him before? A. No, but I have made him a coat since - he found his own cloth.
GEORGE DYER. This note was paid into the Bank on the 3rd of August; it is No. 27,281, 15th of June, 1829.
JOHN REAHDEN. This is the note I received from the prisoner; I had no other customer of the name of Nettleton, and had no other note at all.
RICHARD WOOD. I am a publican. I changed a 5l. note for the prisoner; I did not observe whether there was any name on it, but I wrote on it, "Little gentleman, in the morning;" this is it - it is No. 6116; I cannot say when it was.
GEORGE DYER. This note was paid into the Bank, and is dated the 4th of June.
MR. CRESWELL to RICHARD WOOD. Q. Had you known the prisoner? A. Yes, but did not know his name- he was in the habit of coming to our house; I often change notes for other persons, and occasionally for personsI do not know - if it was for a man six feet high I should put "Tall gentleman."
GEORGE DYER. The other note is No. 24961, and is dated the 6th of June, 1829; it is indorsed
"W. Nettledon, August 4."
WILLIAM BROOKS. ESQ. I am the present treasurer of the school. I have seen the prisoner write frequently - this is his writing on the note, I am satisfied.
Cross-examined. Q. You, I believe, charged the prisoner with this offence? A. Yes, and I wrote him a note, to state in writing what he knew of this transaction; I am not certain whether he answered the first note, or whether I wrote a second; I have not his answer, but the tenor of it was that he had received the cheque from the clerk of the Ironmongers' Company, got it cashed at Hankeys, and took the notes home with him, brought them to town the next day, and enclosed them in an envelope, took it to Mr. Blades, and left it with Mr. Crook and Mr. Jones, who were sitting at the desk together.
Prisoner's Defence. I should have had some friends in the evening, but they were not aware of the trial; they could have witnessed my having received the order of Mr. Blades - I have been at the school since June, 1828, I was sent for by Mr. Blades, in June, 1829, and had not been twelve months at the school, and had received no money - he gave me an order to receive this money from Mr. Pellatt, and hold it till he saw me again; I presented the order at the Hall, and was told it was not in a course of payment; and before it could be paid, I must have a list of the subscribers to the school, and the number of children; I applied at Mr. Blades' for them - Mr. Crook told me he would speak to Mr. Brooks, and send them to me; I waited several days and called several times; Mr. Crook and Mr. Jones know that I have called, and left several letters there, the contents of which they did not know; I have no one to prove that I paid the money - I was engaged by Mr. Brook's as schoolmaster, and by no other person.
MR. BROOKS. I sent the prisoner to the school; he came from a public establishment; and I had confidence in him.
JURY to MR. DEVEY. Q. Who paid the prisoner? A. He was paid by an order from the committee of superintendence on the treasurer.
GUILTY. Aged 40.
Recommended to Mercy by Mr. Devey, having a wife and six children.
Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18300415-351]
1017. WILLIAM HORNE was indicted for breaking and entering the warehouse of Charles Hancock, on the 22nd of February and stealing, 713lbs. of tea, value 300l., his property.
CHARLES HANCOCK. I am a tea-dealer, and live in Leadenhall-street. On Monday morning, the 22nd of February, I came to town from my country house, about ten o'clock in the morning, and heard that my warehouse had been broken open and robbed - it is in the parish of St. Catharine, Creechurch; and is not a part of my dwelling-house -I went there and missed the tea stated, which had been kept in large cans; the cost price of it was 293l. 17s. 10d. and the weight 797lbs.: I saw the cans were empty - but the thieves brought their own bags; there was some tea in bags of mine in the warehouse - but they had not been removed.
EDWARD DAVIES. I am warehouseman to the prosesecutor; no persons sleeps there. On the Saturday evening I had been at the warehouse at six o'clock -I locked it up; I am sure it was secure - the outer door had a lock and a padlock; I went there at eight o'clock on Monday morning, and found the lock of the outer door had been broken, and the hasp of the padlock had been wrenched, so that any one could get in - I did not observe any other mark of violence on the door: I went up three or four steps, and there was another door which had been broken open, and then a third door, which gave access to the tea - I missed some, but I cannot say how much; there were twelve, and ten of them had been emptied - they held 90lbs. or 100lbs. in each; a great quantity was spilled about - I crossed the yard to Mr. Brandon's cellar; it goes by the name of Brandon's warehouse - we occupy one wing and Mr. Brandon the other; when I went to Mr. Brandon's cellar it was about half-past eight o'clock - I found in the cellar three bags, with tea in them, to the weight of 232lbs., and a little further on was the window or door-place, from which the egress was made: there is an opening into Creechurch-lane, but the principal entrance is from Leadenball-street - I compared what I found in the cellar with that scattered about; it appeared to be the same- I saw the bags of tea the same day, about nine or ten o'clock, in possession of the officer; there were seven bags, and 80lbs. or 90lbs. in each - the prisoner was then in custody.
ABRAHAM MOSES. I am a fishmonger, and live in Creechurch-lane, almost opposite the warehouses. I came down about half-past six o'clock that morning, and saw a cart loading at the warehouse-wall; the prisoner and another man were putting bags into the cart, but I only saw two come out of the middle warehouse window, even with the street - I never saw any thing brought out that way before - I did not notice how many bags were in the cart, but there must have been five besides the two I saw put in, as when it was stopped, there were seven in it; two more young men came out of the cellar window, and went away with the other person who was with the prisoner - the prisoner went on in the cart, but was taken before he got to the London Tavern; he went along Leadenhall-street, and I gave information - he appeared to be the driver; the other three men got away, and the prisoner got into the cart and rode off - he had assisted in putting the two bags into the cart.
Cross-examined. Q. How far were you from the men? A. Not above twenty yards, at my own door; the door was open, and the prisoner was carman - I did not hear the others give the prisoner any directions; they spoke together about two minutes.
WILLIAM PLAISTOW. I am an officer of Aldgateward. I received the information when the cart had just got beyond St. Mary-axe - I pursued and came up with it; the prisoner only was with it - I stopped the horse; the prisoner was taken by my brother officer - he had no opportunity of leaving the cart; he said he was hired by two or three men to go and load in Creechurch-lane - he did not say who the men were, but he was to meet them by Bishopsgate-church; he did not know where the things were to go to - there were seven bags of tea in it, which weighed 499.lbs; I know thewarehouse perfectly well - he was not going the nearest way to Bishopsgate-church; he said he was hired, and was told to come the back way to the warehouse - it was a small chaise cart.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you say a word before the Magistrate about the back way? A. No; I was not asked what he said - he did not immediately say he was hired; it might be five minutes - it was at the watch-house he said so; he was not asked on the spot in my presence - we passed Creechurch-lane; he did not point out the spot to me - he said he was hired to take a load from a warehouse in Creechurch lane.
WILLIAM ARTHUR. I apprehended the prisoner in Bishopsgate-street, near the London-tavern; I do not know that that would have been a round-about way to go to Bishopsgate-church, because there is no way of turning the cart in Creechurch-lane; I asked what he had got in the cart - he said he did not know; I saw the name of William Horne, No. 5, Hall's-place, John's-row, St. Luke's, No. 166,780, on the cart - I asked him this question before I took him to the watch-house; Plaistow was at the horse's head, but I cannot say whether he heard it - we were nearly out of breath at the time: we took the cart to the watch-house, and found seven bags of tea in it - he said three men were to meet him at Bishopsgate-church, but he had never seen them before; he owned the horse and cart were his.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it at the watch-house, or before you got there that you asked him this? A. As soon as I took him - I did not ask him again at the watch-house; the other officer went out several times, but I staid there all the time he was at the horse's head. and I took the prisoner some yards from the cart - we did not go back to the watch-house together; he never said at the watch-house that three men had hired him, and told him to go to Bishopsgate-church - he told me so when I stopped him; I think he pointed down Creechurch-lane - as we were going down Leadenhall-street. he did not mention the name of the lane; the other officer was leading the horse - I did not go to Bishopsgate-church.
COURT. Q. How long was the prisoner at the watch-house? A. About half an hour before he was taken to the Magistrate; I did not hear any one enquire about the tea.
COURT to WILLIAM PLAISTOW. Q. Did you hear of any one enquiring after this tea? A. No. except an Excise-officer, who came within a quarter of an hour.
- I am an Excise-officer. I saw the bags of tea; in consequence of what we heard, it was permitted to be returned into stock - the whole weighed 484 lbs.; the bags weighed 14lbs.
Prisoner's Defence. I was hired on Sunday evening at the Three Jolly Weavers, in John's-row, St. Luke's, between seven and eight o'clock.
GEORGE PARGITER. I live at No. 7, Garden-row, Brick-lane, Old-street, and am a chair-maker. The prisoner is a carman, and removes goods; I have seen his cart- it has his name and address on it. On Sunday evening, the 21st of February, I was at the Jolly Weavers, in John's-row, St. Luke's, and saw the prisoner there; I was drinking a pint of beer with a friend, named William, and smoking a pipe - it was between seven and eight o'clock; a man came and asked if a person named Horne was there - the prisoner said, "My name is Horne;" he was a genteel dressed man, and said, "I understand you remove goods?" Horne replied, "I do;" he asked if his cart was engaged for the morning - Horne replied No: I heard no more.
COURT. Q. Where was Horne at that time, in the same box with you? A. No; he was not in our company at all- I have known him about eighteen months to the best of my knowledge; only one person made this enquiry - I dare say I should know the person again, but I had not seen him before nor since; I was not in Court when the prisoner made his defence.
JOHN WILLIAMS. I am a watchmaker, in business for myself, and live in John's-row; I have known the prisoner twelve or thirteen years, living in the neighbourhood - I was at the public-house on that Sunday evening with Pargiter; no one else was in our company - I remember Horne being there; Pargiter had had a pipe, and I was smoking - a man came in and asked for the name of Horne; the prisoner said, "My name is Horne," and he asked if his horse and cart were disengaged for the morning - he said they were; he was a young man of respectable appearance.
COURT. Q. Did you hear any thing more? A. No, nor any particulars of where the things were to be taken from, or to; I did not go before the Magistrate - I said I would come if it was requested, and they said they did not think it would be necessary; I said so to his wife and family.
JOHN McQUISE. The prisoner lived at my house at the time this happened, and was there at six o'clock in the morning, on the day he was taken up; he keeps a horse and cart - I heard a man calling for him, but I did not see him; I did not hear the knock at the door - I heard the prisoner come down; he kept his horse and cart in my yard, and I heard it go out through the passage.
JURY to MR. HANCOCK. Q. Was it a glazed window? A. No, two doors, which open outwards, and are secured inside.
GUILTY. Aged 29. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18300415-352]
Third London Jury - Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1018. REBECCA BRADSHAW and ESTHER BRADSHAW were indicted, for stealing, on the 1st of April, 7 handkerchiefs, value 12s., the goods of Thomas Bech.
THOMAS BECH. I am a silk-mercer, and live in Bishopsgate-street. My young woman stood behind me, while I was showing some handkerchiefs to the prisoners, and after she had stood some time, she gave me some information, and not seeing some handkerchiefs which I knew I had laid on the counter, I charged the prisoners with taking them - they denied it; I sent for a constable- my young woman searched and found the handkerchiefs on them; these are my property - I believe there were no other persons in the shop at the time; I am not quite certain whether a little boy was not there.
SARAH HEATHER. On the 1st of April I was in the employ of the prosecutor; the prisoners came in, and he showed them some handkerchiefs - I saw Rebecca Bradshaw take the handkerchiefs off the counter, and secrete them under her clothes: I did not see the othertouch them, but she was close to her - I told what I had seen; the constable was sent for - he directed me to search Rebecca Bradshaw; I found the handkerchiefs concealed on her - she gave no account of them - they were perhaps a quarter of an hour in the shop - they asked for silk handkerchiefs.
Prisoner Rebecca Bradshaw. This prisoner went to pay off something, not to buy any thing. Witness. No she did not.
JOHN BROWN. I am an officer. I went and found the property on Rebecca Bradshaw; I directed the witness to take it from her - there were five handkerchiefs in one piece, and two in the other; Rebecca said the other knew nothing of it - I found 8 3/4d. on them.
Prisoner Rebecca Bradshaw. I trust you will have mercy on me; it was distress - this female is innocent.
REBECCA BRADSHAW - GUILTY. Aged 26.
ESTHER BRADSHAW - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-353]
1019. JOHN TURNER was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of William Richard Morris, from his person.
WILLIAM RICHARD MORRIS. I was in Fleet-street on the evening of the 7th of April - I felt a pull at my pocket, turned round, and a gentleman had the prisoner and the handkerchief in his hand, and charged him with taking it- he denied it; I had been walking arm-in-arm with a friend - this is my handkerchief.
GILES STEVENS. I was in Fleet-street, and saw the prisoner draw the handkerchief from that gentleman's pocket; I took hold of him.
Prisoner. Q. Were there not several persons about? A. There were two of his companions behind him, and he was close behind the prosecutor; I detained him, and took him to the watch-house.
THOMAS WITHCOMB. I am an officer; I received the prisoner, and have the handkerchief.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been to Whitechapel to see my sister - I came up with these gentlemen; I stepped off the edge of the payement to pass them, and the gentleman caught hold of me.
GUILTY. Aged 26. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-354]
1010. JAMES HANDS was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of Thomas Moriah Otton, from his person.
THOMAS MORIAH OTTON. I lost my handkerchief at Smithfield-bars, on the 15th of March; I was not aware of it till the officer told me of it - I then missed it; I had not seen the prisoner.
JOHN TREE. I was in Smithfield on Monday morning, the 15th of March; I saw the prisoner and two other boys following this gentleman - I got as close as I could, and saw the prisoner draw the handkerchief from this gentleman's pocket, and pass it to another boy, who got away; I took the prisoner, and got him through the crowd - I told the prosecutor of it.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going through Smithfield, and this gentleman accused me of robbing a person of his handkerchief; I knew nothing of it.
GUILTY. Aged 15. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-355]
1011. ELIZA WATSON was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of April, 42 yards of silk, value 4l., and 1 roller, value 1d., the goods of Thomas Hall.
CHARLES MILLER. I am assistant to Mr. Thomas Hall, linen-draper, Bishopsgate-street. On the 12th of April, about five o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came into the shop and stood by the side of another customer; she asked for nothing, but the roll of silk was lying on the counter - she had a linen cloth in her hand, she laid it on the silk, and took it out; I followed her just outside the door - I took her with it; she confessed it directly - this is it; it has the private mark on it.
THOMAS SAPWELL. This silk was given to me.
The prisoner put in a petition pleading poverty. She received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury. - Confined 1 Month.
[trialAccount t18300415-356]
1012. THOMAS CONNELL was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March, 1 coat, value 4s., the goods of James Sadler.
JAMES SADLER. On the 26th of March I had been on an errand, and on coming back I saw the prisoner at the further end of the passage; he took my coat off the hook and put it under his apron - when he was almost out of the door I called Mr. Curry, who came and took him.
Prisoner. I went in to ask for a person who lodged there, and a boy ran against me and dropped the coat. Witness. No, that is not true; he asked if it belonged to any one in the house, after I had called Mr. Curry.
JOSEPH CURRY. I was called, and took the coat from under his apron; he said he was very sorry, and would not do it again - I took him to the watch-house.
JOHN PEARCE. I took the prisoner; I have the coat -I found a pocket-book and some duplicates upon him.
Prisoner's Defence. A boy ran against me and dropped the coat; I said, "Does this belong to any body in the house?" - this lad said, "It belongs to me;" I said take it - this witness said to the boy, "If you say every word I tell you we shall get 7s. a day, every day we attend; you know the state your mother is in, and I am out of work" - it is for 7s. a day that the lad perjures himself; I have had the best of characters - Major Dod left me 700l. and now I have 1200l. to receive from Government; if I get away I shall not be in England three days.
JOHN SADLER. I did not hear him say any such thing.
JOHN PEARCE. Nothing of the kind happened in my presence.
GUILTY. Aged 72. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18300415-357]
1013. BENJAMIN BINDON was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 6d., the goods of Thomas Hall, from his person.
THOMAS HALL. I was close against St. Dunstan's-church, in Fleet-street; a person touched me. and asked if I had lost any thing - I felt my pocket and missed my handkerchief, which I had put there but a few minutes before the officer produced it; it is a very old one, and before I went out, I had torn this piece which I have here from one of the corners.
GEORGE POWELL. I gave the prisoner into custody to the City officer - I saw him put his hand into his pocket; he then fell back and put this handkerchief into his insidegreat coat pocket, and when I seized him he took it out and threw it down - I did not see him take the handkerchief from the prosecutor, but he took it from his own pocket and threw it down; he endeavoured to get away -I tried to pick up the handkerchief, and he kicked me.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going home - that young man accused me of picking the gentleman's pocket, which is a falsity.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Transported for 14 Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-358]
1014. ISABELLA FELL was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 3 handkerchiefs, value 6s., the goods of William Stephen Dew and another.
HORATIO MACKLIN. I am in the employ of William Stephen Dew and Co. On the 6th of April the prisoner came into our shop; he took up three handkerchiefs from the side of our counter, and walked out - I followed her and found them on her; they are the property of my employers; the prisoner said nothing - she had not bargained for any thing; there were other persons in the shop.
THOMAS DAVIES. I am an officer. I took the prisoner and have the property - she told me the next day that she would not have done it had she been sober, but I did not observe that she was tipsy; she said her husband had left her.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in great distress, and was without bread for two days before I made the rash attempt.
HORATIO MACKLIN. It was on the counter inside the door.
GUILTY. Aged 28. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18300415-359]
1015. ISABELLA COWLING and MARGARET BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of April, I half-sovereign, the monies of John Scott, from his person.
JOHN SCOTT. I am servant to Mr. Jackson, coal-merchant, at Dock-head. On the 10th of April I was on Fish-street-hill, about eleven o'clock at night - I met Cowling, and walked on with her till I met Brown, which was in about ten minutes; I then put my hand into my pocket, felt my half-sovereign and buttoned up my pocket - I then missed my half-sovereign - Brown went away; I took hold of the other till I found whether my pockets were picked - both their hands had been in my pockets, one hand in each; I called the watchman, who came and brought his lantern - I thought I might have lost it; the watchman looked about, and I gave charge of Cowling - the other was taken afterwards.
ROBERT DICK. I am inspector of the Ward of Bishops-gate. I saw the prosecutor with Cowling - he charged her with stealing a half-sovereign, and said there had been another woman in company and described her - I went and took Brown and took hold of her hand to prevent her throwing any thing away; I found two half-crowns in her hand, which she said she received from a gentleman in Cavendish-court - the prosecutor had been drinking, but was not so far gone but I think his testimony might be taken.
EDWARD PRATT. I am a night officer. I searched Cowling, and took 1s. 6d. and 3 1/2d. in copper from her - from Brown I took 4s. and 1 1/2d. in copper.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300415-360]
1016. WILLIAM SULLIVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 1 handkerchief, value 5s., the goods of Matthew Phillips, from his person.
MATTHEW PHILLIPS. On the 20th of February I was on Holborn-hill looking at a horse which had fallen; I felt something behind me, and missed my handkerchief - I saw the prisoner making off; I followed and took him with the handkerchief in his breast.
HENRY BERESFORD. I received the prisoner and the handkerchief - he said at the office, that some other boy had shoved it into his hand.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. There were other boys about? A. I think there might have been time to do that - he had it in his hand, and was just buttoning up his coat.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going on an errand to Clare-market - I went to look at the horse; just as I came out of the mob a boy shoved it into my hand - I put it in my breast and was taken.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18300415-361]
1017. JAMES SHEARMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March. 1 table-spoon, value 16s., and 1 fork, value 7s., the goods of John Abbott and others, his masters.
JOHN ABBOTT. I am in partnership with four other persons, who keep the City of London tavern : the prisoner was our apprentice. On the 29th of March we missed a fork and a spoon, and on the 30th I found the prisoner in the passage with one of our porters - he pretended to be fainting, and the fork fell from his jacket; I told the porter to feel up his trousers, and he felt the spoon - I sent him into the hall, and the porter said there had been a man with him in the street; I said, "Come to the door, and point him out to me;" he went and said, "That is the man;" I sent for him - he came in, and I asked him to write his address down; he wrote his mother's address, and said his father was electioneering - I took the prisoner to the Mansion-house; he said he was going to pawn these articles to pay his brother 2s.
CHARLES KNIGHT. I am a porter at the tavern. On the 30th of March, between six and seven o'clock in the morning, my fellow-servant in the kitchen told me he saw a spoon and a fork behind the dresser; I said, "We will not take them away till we see who goes out;" and the first that went out was the prisoner - then we looked, and saw they were removed; I went, and found the prisoner in the street, and brought him back - the fork dropped from him in the passage, and I found the spoon up his trousers.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not take them with intent to steal them.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18300415-362]
1018. WILLIAM GLASSON was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 1 coat, value 1l. 10s., and 1 waistcoat, value 10s., the goods of Thomas Chandler.
THOMAS CHANDLER. I lost a coat and waistcoat on the 13th of March, from a counting-house in Little Tower-street, belonging to my employer - I was attracted by the shutting of the counting-house door, and on looking through a window I saw the prisoner with the coat and waistcoat on his arm - I ran out, he dropped them, and hesitated which way he should go; he turned to the left; I went, and took him, when he had ran about fifty yards.
Prisoner's Defence. I was very ill, and had been in the hospital - I had my doctor's bill to pay.
GUILTY. Aged 56. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18300415-363]
1019. JOHN JAMES was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of William Holland, from his person.
WILLIAM HOLLAND. On the 19th of March I went to Smithfield to select a horse, and while trying him in a cart I was pushed about, and felt a pull at my pocket - I put my hand, and missed my handkerchief, which I had safe five minutes before; it was then half-past three o'clock in the afternoon - the prisoner was going towards the pens- I followed, and took him; he was doubling up this handkerchief, which is mine.
THOMAS BROWN. I saw the prisoner take the handkerchief from the prosecutor on the 19th of March - he rolled it under his apron, walked away, and the prosecutor took him with it.
Prisoner. It was ten minutes before they took me.
MR. HOLLAND. I took him directly they came to rescue him - one man said he was an officer, but I would not let him go.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18300415-364]
1020. JAMES JEFFERY was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 6d., the goods of Robert Penfold.
ROBERT PENFOLD. On the 15th of April, about ten minutes past seven in the morning, I was unloading a coal-waggon in Tower-street; I took off my hat, and my handkerchief was in it - I put it on the premises while I went down with a sack of coals; when I came up a gentleman's son said, "Either you, or your mate, have lost a handkerchief;" and he showed me the prisoner in Mark-lane - there were four persons in company; he was the stoutest of them - I took him, and found my handkerchief in his bosom; this is it - I know it by a darn, and by the pattern: I gave it to the officer - it was as I had pulled it off my neck; I suppose I found it on him in about twenty minutes after I first put it down - we had unloaded one waggon; the prisoner's companions had parted from him, before I got up to him.
Prisoner's Defence. It was given me by the three men, who told me to put it round my neck; I put it in my bosom, and gave it to the gentleman.
JOHN THOMPSON. I took charge of the prisoner - he said it was given to him by one of the three men he had come from Greenwich fair with.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-365]
1021. ANN THOMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of March, 14 sovereigns, and three 10l. notes, the property of Richard Heaven, from his person; and GEORGE THOMPSON was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, knowing them to be stolen.
RICHARD HEAVEN. On the 15th of March I had three 10l. notes and fourteen sovereigns in a purse in my breeches-pocket - about ten o'clock at night I met Ann Thompson in Thames-street, and went with her down an alley leading round to the Shades; we stopped two or three minutes at the bottom of the alley, and she gave me a slap on the face - my hat fell off; I caught her shawl and the shawl, came off - my purse was gone; I observed her particularly, and can swear she is the person - she got away; the officer has part of the money, and the pawnbroker has a 10l. note - I could swear to the purse, but that has not been found.
JOHN HACKETT. I was sent for from the Grapes public-house between ten and eleven o'clock - the prosecutor said he had lost 44l., and he gave me this shawl, which he said he took from this woman's shoulders; I knew I had seen this woman with it commonly in the street, and I went with my partner the next day to a lodging where she had lived, but we could not find her - I went to a pawnbroker, and said if a woman named Thompson came there they were to stop her; I know nothing of the man.
WILLIAM SOWERBY. I am a pawnbroker. I have a 10l. note, which I got from the female prisoner early in the morning of the 16th of March, she offered it to pay for some duplicates - having heard of this robbery; I sent for an officer and detained her.
WILLIAM LAND. I got change of a check for the prosecutor on the 8th of March, in three 10l. notes and twenty sovereigns; I went to the banking-house on the 16th of March and got the numbers of the notes.
MARY WEBB. I am niece of Mr. Rushmore, who keeps the White Bear. On the 16th of March the male prisoner came and had some liquor, and left two 10l. notes and nine sovereigns with me - he said it was a legacy left him in the country; he went out returned, and asked for them, and I gave them to him.
DAVID HEALEY. On the 16th of March information was given at our office; I went and found the male prisoner at No. 73, Lambeth-street, in the privy - I was about to search him, and he dropped two 10l. notes and nine sovereigns; a person who is not here picked them up and gave them to me - he said they did not belong to him, and he knew nothing about them; I then searched him and found 18s.6 1/4d. on him, and 16s. 6 1/4d. on the woman.
THOMAS RIGG. I am cashier at Key and Co.'s, I paid the witness three 10l. notes, Nos. 2882, 2883, and 2884; dated the 13th of February, 1820.
WILLIAM LAND. I gave the prosecutor the same notes which I had from the banking-house.
MR. HEAVEN. I lost the same notes as I got from Land these are them.
Ann Thompson's Defence. I never set my eyes on the man till I was at Worship-street; I picked up that money on the pavement as I was returning home after selling my goods, I kicked against it, and then picked it up; there were nine sovereigns and three notes - I could not read, I went home and then went to the public-house; I told my husband I had picked up some money - I went to the pawnbroker's next morning to redeem some clothes with the note, ignorant like; the 16s. 6d. I had taken for my goods, I never had that shawl.
George Thomspon 's Defence. My wife came home and said,
"I have been picking up a lot of money." and I found it was three 10l. notes and more sovereigns; the 18s. 6d. found on me was for three pairs of water-proof boots I had made.
ANN THOMPSON - GUILTY. Aged 26.
Transported for Life.
GEORGE THOMPSON - GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-366]
1022. THOMAS HAYES was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Richard Wallace, from his person.
RICHARD WALLACE. I was in Fleet-street, opposite Chancery-lane, at half-past seven o'clock on the evening in question, and felt some one at my pocket; I sprang round, and caught the prisoner putting my handkerchief under his jacket - I had been walking with my wife and sister; it has my initials, and No. 3 on it - this is it; there were several persons passing, but none in his company.
THOMAS BETTS. I saw the prosecutor take the handkerchief from the prisoner's jacket, as he was tucking it up.
THOMAS LIGHTFOOT. I am an officer. I produce the handkerchief; Lightfoot, who took it, broke his leg yesterday.
GUILTY. Aged 14. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-367]
1023. JOSEPH JENNINGS was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of January, 7 shoes, value 5s., the goods of Peter Welch, his master.
PETER WELCH. I am in the boot and shoe line, in Field-lane. The prisoner was in my employ; I sent him with three pairs and an odd shoe to take to the binders, on the 23rd of January, and never saw him again till the 24th of March, when I saw him by St. Bride's, Fleet-street; I asked what he had done with my shoes - he said he knew nothing about me; I said, "Is not your name Joseph Jennings" - he said No; I asked if his name was his step-father's name - he said No; I asked where he lived - he said in Winkfield-street; I said I would go there - in going along he said he had sold the shoes in Playhouse-yard; I went there, and the house was shut up - I then gave him in charge of an officer; a better boy than he was, was not in England before.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-368]
1024. CHARLES BRIGG was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 1 printed bound book, value 2s., the goods of William Jeffery.
WILLIAM JEFFERY. I am a bookseller, and live in Mark-lane. I lost a book on the 20th of February from a tray inside my window, which opens with a sash; I had seen it in the morning, perhaps eight hours before it was taken - this is it; it is Virgil, in Latin.
JAMES BOWEN. I was standing in my master's shop, and observed the prisoner - I was told he had taken a book; I went after him - he saw me, and threw the book away up a court; he ran off, but was stopped immediately - I had seen the book about five minutes before.
JAMES HALL. I saw the prisoner running, and throw this book down; I took him from a young man who stopped him.
Prisoner's Defence. I saw the book on the pavement some distance from the shop - I took it up; this lad hallooed, and I threw it down.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 34.
Recommended to Mercy. - Fined 1s. and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18300415-369]
1025. JOHN RICHARDSON was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of March, 1 horse-cloth, value 8s., the goods of Robert Gray.
CHARLES MONK. I am foreman to Mr. Robert Gray, a coach-master, at the Bolt-in-Tun, Fleet-street. On the 14th of March, a horse-cloth was taken from the stables; I had put it on the horse at eleven o'clock, but I did not see it taken; I took the prisoner with it in Fetter-lane -I knew him; he had been employed there.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GEORGE SHARP. I am an officer. I took the prisoner at the Bolt-in-Tun tap.
Prisoner's Defence. I was very tipsy.
GUILTY. Aged 38. - Fined One Shilling and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18300415-370]
1026. JOHN ROBINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 3s., the goods of Robert Harris, from his person.
ROBERT HARRIS. On the 16th of March I was waiting to cross Queen-street, Cheapside, about half-past eleven o'clock - I did not feel my handkerchief taken, but the officer touched me; I turned, and saw him with the prisoner and this handkerchief in his hand.
JOHN ROE. I am an officer. I saw the prisoner half a dozen yards on this side Queen-street, with Brown; I saw the prisoner's hand go towards the prosecutor's person, and then saw him putting something under his coat - I went and took him with this handkerchief; I did not see it come from the prosecutor - I believe he was drawn into it - he was not known at all.
Prisoner's Defence. It being my first offence, I hope you will take it into your consideration.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury.
Fined One Shilling and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18300415-371]
1027. GEORGE MATTHEWS was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of February, 11 handkerchiefs, value 1ls., the goods of William Bradley.
FRANCIS EDWARDS. I am in the employ of Mr. William Bradley, of Newgate-street. On the 23rd of February the prisoner came in, and plucked eleven handkerchiefs from a wooden rail inside the shop; I ran, and took him near the Compter, in Giltspur-street, with the property - I gave him into custody; they are the property of Mr. William Bradley.
THOMAS PYKE. I am the officer. I took the prisoner, and have the property.
CHARLES HOPPE. I was crossing the street, and saw the prisoner draw them from the inside of the shop.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18300415-372]
1028. WILLIAM NORTON was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of March, 1 pair of half-boots, value 8s. 6d., the goods of Zachariah Foster.
ZACHARIAH FOSTER. I am a boot and shoemaker, and live in Newgate-street. I lost a pair of half-boots from about two feet within the door of my shop, on the 8th of March - I saw the prisoner take them and run away; I ran after him, and took him with them.(Property produced and sworn to.)
JOSEPH BATES. I have the boots, and took the prisoner.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-373]
1029. WILLIAM STACEY was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of a certain person, whose name is unknown, from his person.
EDWARD CORKER. I am a gentleman's coachman. On the 7th of April I was down Crutched-friars, and saw a gentleman and two ladies arm-in-arm - I saw the prisoner draw a handkerchief from the gentleman's pocket; he had it in his hand - the gentleman ran after him, called Stop thief! and another gentleman stopped him; the officer took him into custody - I did not know the gentleman.
THOMAS DEVEY. I am an officer. I took the prisoner- I saw him run up Crutched-friars; a gentleman pursued him, and threw an umbrella at him - the gentleman would not give his address; this is the handkerchief - the prisoner threw it away, and said he did not take it.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18300415-374]
1030. JOHN MORRIS was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of February, 1 watch, value 2l.; 1 watch-key, value 6d., and 1 watch-ribbon, value 6d., the goods of Charles Markham, from his person.
CHARLES MARKHAM. On the 24th of February I was against the Rose-inn, Smithfield, at a quarter-past two o'clock; the prisoner came running up, drew my watch out of my pocket, and ran down West-street; I followed, and overtook him, without losing sight of him - I saw him throw the watch away, but I got it.
JAMES SAVILLE. I took the prisoner on the 24th of February, at twenty minutes after three o'clock - I saw a crowd outside the door of an eating-house - I went up, and they said, "You are wanted here - a man has been robbed of his watch;" I went in, and the prosecutor had the prisoner.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18300415-375]
1031. PETER JAMES BOSSY was indicted for wilful and corrupt perjury; to which he pleaded
GUILTY. Aged 31.
Confined Six Months, to stand in the Pillory, and then to be Transported for Seven Years.
